Page 1 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard CLEVELAND-CLIFFS STEEL Confined Space Safety Standard Effective Date: 11-3-2023 Supersedes: All Previous Versions Standard No.: CCS_HSS_STD_004.2 Follow-Up Review Date: 11-3-2024 1 Purpose/Scope……………………………………………………. 2 2 Responsibilities and Authorities………………………………. 3 3 References and Definitions…………………………………….. 4 4 Resources…………………………………………………………. 9 5 Requirements……………………………………………………... 10 6 Forms and Documented Information…………………………. 21 7 Opportunities and Risks………………………………………… 21 8 Revision History………………………………………………….. 22 Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 2 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard 1 Purpose/Scope 1.1 This Standard has been authorized and approved to establish the general procedures necessary to eliminate, control, prevent unauthorized access to, or mitigate the safety and health hazards unique to confined spaces. It is based on developing specific procedures for each space. 1.2 This Confined Space Standard applies to Cleveland-Cliffs Steel personnel and to all contractors who may enter a confined space on Cleveland-Cliffs Steel properties. 1.3 The Take 5 process is an important part of all tasks we perform and whenever possible we shall use this process to minimize the need to enter a confined space. Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 3 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard 2 Responsibilities and Authorities 2.1 This standard was jointly developed with participation from the represented workforce and management. 2.2 Each facility’s management is responsible for the implementation of this standard. 2.3 The Senior Director of Health, Safety and Security is responsible for authorizing this standard. 2.4 Confined Space Entry Procedures must be reviewed and revised as necessary. This must be done: 2.4.1 Annually or before the next entry. 2.4.2 Following any unplanned event. 2.4.3 After any change which may affect the entry. 2.5 It is the responsibility of the department \"owning\" or controlling the confined space to maintain the Confined Space Entry Procedure, keep canceled permits and keep contractor notification and debriefing records for at least one year. 2.6 The Confined Space Entry Program and entry procedures are subject to annual review and periodic audit to ensure proper program direction and execution. 2.7 Each closed permit will be reviewed by the department and filed in a way that allows review for a period of 1 year. 2.7.1 Each department must review their program annually. Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 4 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard 3 References and Definitions 3.1 References • 29 CFR 1910.146 • 29 CFR 1926.1200 • MIOSHA-STD-1160 3.2 Definitions Attendant: An individual stationed outside a confined space who monitors the authorized entrants and performs all the attendant's duties assigned in the confined space program. The Attendant will be assigned no other duties that prevent the accomplishment of the duties required. Acceptable Entry Conditions: The conditions that must exist in a permit space to allow entry and to ensure that employees involved with a permit-required confined space entry can safely enter and work within the space. Alternate Entry Procedures: Procedures that may be used when the only hazard of a confined space, based upon monitoring and inspection data, is an actual or potential hazardous atmosphere in which continuous forced air ventilation alone is all that is needed to maintain the Permit Required Confined Space for safe entry. Authorized Entrant: An employee who is authorized by the employer to enter a permit required confined space. Blanking or Blinding: The absolute closure of a pipe, line, or duct by the fastening of a solid plate (such as a spectacle blind or a skillet blind) that completely covers the bore and that can withstand the maximum pressure of the pipe, line or duct with no leakage beyond the plate. Competent Person: One who can identify existing and predictable hazards in the surroundings or working conditions which are unsanitary, hazardous, or dangerous to employees, and who has the authorization to take prompt corrective measures to eliminate them. ∆ Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 5 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard Confined Space: A space that: • Is large enough and so configured that an employee can bodily enter and perform assigned work. • Has limited or restricted means for entry or exit (for example, tanks, vessels, storage bins, hoppers, vaults, pits, ladles, and sewers that may have limited means of entry). • Is not designed for continuous occupancy. Double Block and Bleed: The closure of a line, duct, or pipe by closing and locking two in-line valves, and by opening and locking a drain or vent valve in the line between the two closed valves. Emergency: Any occurrence (including any failure of hazard control or monitoring equipment) or event(s), internal or external, to the confined space that could endanger entrants. Engulfment: The surrounding and effective capture of a person by a liquid or finely divided (flowable) solid substance that can be aspirated to cause death by filling or plugging the respiratory system, or that can exert enough force on the body to cause death by strangulation, constriction or crushing. Entrant: An employee authorized by the entry supervisor to enter a confined space. Entrapment: The capture and potential asphyxiation of a person by being trapped in a confined space. • With inwardly converging walls. • A floor which slopes downward or tapers to a smaller cross section. Entry: The action by which a person passes through an opening into a confined space. Entry includes ensuing work activities in that space and is considered to have occurred as soon as any part of the entrant's body breaks the plane of an opening into the space. Entry Supervisor: The person responsible for determining if acceptable entry conditions are present at a permit space where entry is planned, for authorizing entry and overseeing entry operations, and for terminating entry. Entry Permit System: The employer's written procedures for preparing and issuing permits for entry and returning the permit space to service following termination of entry and designates by name or title the individuals who may authorize entry. Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 6 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard Entry Permit: The written or printed document that is provided by the employer to allow and control entry into a permit space, and contains the information specified in section 5 of this document. Hazardous Atmosphere: An atmosphere that may expose employees to the risk of death, incapacitation, and impairment of ability to self-rescue (that is, escape unaided from a permit space), injury or acute illness from one or more of the following causes: • Flammable gas, vapor, or mist more than 10 percent of its lower flammable limit (LFL). • Airborne combustible dust at a concentration that meets or exceeds its LFL (dust obscuring vision at 5 feet). • Atmospheric Oxygen concentration below 19.5 percent or above 23.5 percent. • The presence of any substance whose concentration exceeds a recognized permissible exposure limit or dose. For those substances that have no permissible exposure level or dose, refer to other recognized data such as safety data sheets, manufacturers' data, and lab analysis for determining acceptable conditions. • Atmospheric concentration of any substance which may exceed a permissible exposure limit. • Any other atmospheric condition that is immediately dangerous to life or health. Hot Work Permit: The employer's written authorization to perform operations (for example, riveting, welding, cutting, burning, and heating) capable of providing a source of ignition. Immediately Dangerous to Life or Health (IDLH): Any condition that poses an immediate or delayed threat to life, or that would cause irreversible adverse health effects, or that would interfere with an individual's ability to escape unaided from a permit space. Inerting: The displacement of the atmosphere in a permit space by a noncombustible gas (such as nitrogen) to such an extent that the resulting atmosphere is noncombustible. Isolation: The process by which a permit space is removed from service and completely protected against the release of energy and material into the space by such means as: blanking or blinding; misaligning or removing sections of lines, pipes or ducts; a double block and bleed system; lockout of all sources of energy; or blocking or disconnecting all mechanical linkages. Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 7 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard Line Breaking: The intentional opening of a pipe, line or duct that is or has been carrying flammable, corrosive or toxic material, an inert gas, or any fluid at a volume, pressure, or temperature capable of causing injury. Non-Permit Confined Space: A confined space that does not contain, or with respect to atmospheric hazards, have the potential to contain any hazard capable of causing death or serious physical harm. Oxygen Deficient Atmosphere: An atmosphere containing less than 19.5 percent oxygen by volume. Oxygen Enriched Atmosphere: An atmosphere containing more than 23.5 percent oxygen by volume. Permit Required Confined Space: Means a confined space that has one or more of the following characteristics: • Contains or has the potential to contain a hazardous atmosphere. • Contains material that has the potential for engulfment of an entrant. • Has an internal configuration such that an entrant could be trapped or asphyxiated by inwardly converging walls, or a floor which slopes downward and tapers to a smaller cross-section or; • Contains any other recognized serious safety or health hazard. PEL (Permission Exposure Limit): Average concentration that must not be exceeded during an 8-hour work shift of a 40-hour work week. Permit System: The employer's written procedure for preparing and issuing permits for entry, and for returning the permit space to service following termination of entry. Permit Required Confined Space Program: The employer's overall program for controlling, and where appropriate, for protecting employees from permit space hazards and for regulating employee entry into permit spaces. Prohibited Condition: Any condition in a permit space that is not allowed by the permit during the period when entry is authorized. Qualified Person: One who, by possession of a recognized degree, certificate, or professional standing, or who by extensive knowledge, training, and experience, has successfully demonstrated his ability to solve or resolve problems relating to the subject matter, the work or the project. Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 8 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard Retrieval System: The equipment (including a retrieval line, full body harness, wristlets, if appropriate, and a lifting device or anchor) used for non-entry rescue of persons from the permit space. Representative Permit Space: A mock-up of a confined space that has entrance openings that are similar to, and is of similar size, configuration, and accessibility to the permit space that authorized entrants enter. Rescue Service: The personnel designated to rescue employees from permit spaces. • Rescue services need to be qualified and selected to perform rescue services. • Cleveland-Cliffs Steel Safety will determine what contractors are qualified to perform rescue services. Testing: The process by which the hazards that may confront entrants of a permit space are identified and evaluated. Testing includes specifying the tests that are to be performed in the permit space. Testing enables employers both to devise and implement adequate control measures for the protection of authorized entrants and to determine if acceptable entry conditions are present immediately prior to, and during entry. Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 9 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard 4 Resources 4.1 Decision Flowchart Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 10 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard 5 Requirements 5.1 General Responsibilities 5.1.1 Divisions/Departments are responsible for: a. Identifying all confined spaces in their areas. b. Identifying and post signage and maintain signage at all confined spaces. c. Maintain an inventory of all confined spaces. d. Ensuring all personnel who enter confined spaces are trained. e. Implementing this confined space entry program. f. Performing confined space hazard assessments. g. Developing space specific entry procedure. h. Ensure all spaces are maintained closed to prevent unauthorized access. i. Maintain the space so that a tool or the removal of latches or similar methods is needed to gain entry. j. Ensuring that contractor requirements of this program are implemented. k. Ensuring that all contractors who may enter a confined space are made aware of this program. 5.1.2 Health and Safety Department is responsible for: a. Assisting departments in identifying confined spaces. b. Assisting departments in completion of required training. c. Assisting departments with confined space assessments. d. Assisting departments in monitoring people working in confined spaces. e. Auditing the program. Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 11 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard 5.1.3 Contractors are responsible for: a. Adopting the Cleveland-Cliffs confined space program or implementing one that meets or exceeds the Cleveland-Cliffs program. b. Training their employees. c. Supplying their own safety and monitoring equipment. d. Maintaining the permit used for entry. 5.1.4 Entry Alternatives a. When work is assigned, the crew should utilize the Take 5 process to identify if entry into the space will be required. b. Whenever feasible, design changes or work practice will be developed to eliminate the need for entry into confined spaces. c. Non-entry work practices will be covered by a JSA or equivalent methods of standardized work instruction. 5.2 Confined Space 5.2.1 For Cleveland-Cliffs, all locations that meet all the following criteria are to be considered as a CONFINED SPACE: a. Is large enough and so configured that an employee can bodily enter and perform assigned work and, b. Has limited or restricted means for entry or exit (for example, tanks, vessels, storage bins, hoppers, vaults, pits, sewers and roll off containers that may have limited means of entry), c. Is not designed for continuous occupancy. 5.2.2 Permit Required Confined spaces contain at least one of the following: a. Contains, or has the potential to contain, a hazardous atmosphere. b. Contains, or has the potential to contain, flowable material that could engulf. c. Has an internal configuration such that one could be trapped or asphyxiated by an inwardly converging wall or by a floor which slopes downward and tapers to a small cross section. d. Contains any other recognized serious safety or health hazard. (An example of this situation would be exposed, uninsulated electrical wiring.) Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 12 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard 5.2.3 Cleveland-Cliffs requires all confined space entry to utilize an entry permit and be attended as if it were a permitted space. Spaces that meet the definition of a Permit Required space also require rescue services. 5.3 Confined Space Inventory 5.3.1 A complete inventory of all known confined spaces within a department will be made and reviewed annually. Additional spaces will be included upon discovery. It is the responsibility of the department to generate this inventory and keep it current. 5.3.2 This inventory shall be reviewed annually or more frequently as necessary to maintain an accurate inventory. 5.3.3 Made accessible to all employees as needed. 5.3.4 The inventory will uniquely identify each space. a. Department, Unique number, Common Name. Example: Rolling Mill (Space) 29 Pit pumps (RM-29- Pit Pumps) 5.4 Posting 5.4.1 All Employees must be alerted to the presence of the confined space. 5.4.2 Appropriate actions must be taken to prevent unauthorized entry. 5.4.3 All accessible entrances to inventoried confined spaces shall be posted near the entrance with the following: DANGER: CONFINED SPACE AUTHORIZED ENTRY ONLY (or similar language to identify spaces at each facility in a consistent manner.) 5.4.4 All spaces including manholes in roadways will be uniquely identified. 5.4.5 A unique identifier will be posted at each space near the point of entry. 5.4.6 If you discover an area that might be a confined space and it is not marked as such, notify your supervisor. 5.5 Entry Procedures 5.5.1 Safe entry procedures for confined spaces must be developed and written in a standard step-by-step format. Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 13 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard 5.5.2 These step-by-step procedures must be created with enough detail and supporting documentation, such as pictures and diagrams, that a person preparing to enter a confined space can verify that the actions required, and conditions specified do exist and that the actions required have been met. 5.5.3 It is the responsibility of the department controlling the confined space to create, maintain, review, revise and approve the entry procedure. 5.5.4 Approval of new and revised entry procedures must be approved by the Operating Department Division Manager or Section Manager, Maintenance Section Manager., and the Safety Department. a. Confined spaces entered at least once a year must have specific entry procedures, pre-planned and in writing. Whenever possible these must be created in advance of any entry to allow thorough review by operations, maintenance, safety, and entry groups. b. Spaces entered less frequently than once per year shall have their procedures developed prior to entry as soon as the need to enter the space is recognized. 5.5.5 Current copies of any referenced documents must be attached to the Confined Space Permit at the time of use. a. Includes SDS for materials that may already be present in the space, and for materials that entrants might introduce into the space. SDS (Safety Data Sheet) information must be reviewed to assure all hazards are eliminated or controlled. b. Includes energy control procedures. c. Includes hot work permits if necessary. d. For contractors includes Joint Job Hazard Assessment and Controls form 5.6 Confined Space Permit 5.6.1 Confined Space Permit is to be used as written documentation to assure that all hazards or potential hazards are dealt with appropriately. 5.6.2 The Confined Space Permit is the working document completed to gain and maintain an entry into a space. 5.6.3 Until the Confined Space Permit is completed, the Confined Space shall be considered to contain a hazard. ∆ Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 14 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard 5.6.4 Confined Space Entry Permits do not include or take the place of hot work permits. 5.6.5 The Confined Space Entry Permit must contain the following information: a. The date and time of entry. b. The expiration time of the entry. c. The name and location of the permit space. d. The purpose of the entry. e. The hazards of the permit space to be entered and the acceptable entry conditions. f. The identity of each authorized entrant, each attendant, and the entry supervisor. g. The measures to be taken to isolate the permit space and to eliminate or control permit space hazards before entry. h. The results of initial and periodic tests performed with the identity of testers. i. The name and telephone numbers of rescue and emergency services and means to be used to contact them. j. The additional permits, such as for hot work, have been issued authorizing work in the permit space. k. Special equipment and procedures, including PPE (Personal Protective Equipment) and alarm systems. 5.6.6 Confined space permits will expire after 12 hours and must be re- issued. 5.7 Attendant 5.7.1 An attendant is required for any entry into any confined space. 5.7.2 Under no circumstances will the confined space Attendant enter the confined space. 5.8 Entry Supervisor 5.8.1 An entry supervisor is required for every confined space. Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 15 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard 5.9 Rescue 5.9.1 Rescue Standards: a. If hazards within a confined space cannot be eliminated, and if rescue cannot be done from outside the space, or the conditions in the space are Immediately Dangerous to Life and Health (IDLH), a rescue team is required. b. If rescue is provided by a non-Cleveland-Cliffs source, advanced access to the confined space and training on associated hazards must be provided. c. Confined spaces that require rescue teams must have the rescue team and required equipment on-site and within range of the space as pre-determined prior to entry. They must also be in range of the emergency signal or method of summoning the rescue team. d. A rescue team may be utilized for more than one space and does not need to be staged at the entry unless the conditions of entry require one to be immediately available. 5.9.2 Rescue from outside the confined space. a. If it is determined that rescue can be performed from outside the confined space, the person(s) responsible for rescue must be trained in the following: b. Use of the mechanical hoist or other equipment that will be used to retrieve the entrant. c. Any hazards that exist outside of the confined space that could interfere with rescue. d. The necessary emergency numbers and how to access communications. e. Rescue personnel must be provided with quick access to communications equipment so that emergency personnel can be contacted immediately if rescue operations are put into effect. 5.9.3 Non-entry rescue equipment and setup. a. Non-Entry Rescue equipment to be used by trained personnel includes items such as but not limited to a tripod, davit arm and horizontal retrieval device with approved retrieval systems. b. Full body harnesses are to be worn by all entrants going into a confined space when needed to facilitate rescue. ∆ Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 16 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard c. A tripod or similar rescue device will be positioned to the side of vertical confined spaces and checked for stability prior to entry by the attendee and individuals working in the confined space. Once opened, the tripod will be placed over the vertical opening to assure stability and fit for rescue. After checking the tripod, it can be situated to the side of the confined space opening. d. All atmospheric testing and ventilation shall be conducted according to the established confined space program and be posted at/or near the opening of the space. e. Under no circumstances will a crane or powered hoist be used in a rescue operation. 5.10 Duties of Confined Space Participants 5.10.1 Entry team duties. 5.10.2 Duties of authorized entrants: a. Follow entry requirements of the permit. b. Know the hazards that may be faced during entry. c. Wear full body harness when entering the space when needed to facilitate rescue. d. Recognize the signs and symptoms of hazard exposure. e. Understand the consequences of hazardous exposure. f. Use equipment properly. g. Communicate with the attendant. h. Alert the attendant of hazards. i. Exit the permit space immediately when required. j. Wear a personal Four Gas monitor. 5.10.3 Duties of the attendant: a. Know entry hazards. b. Know behavioral effects of exposure to the hazards likely to be present. c. Maintain accurate entrant identification. d. Maintain accurate records on the confined space permit. ∆ Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 17 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard e. Remain outside the permit space. f. Do not leave the space entry until all Entrants are out of the space or you are relieved by another qualified attendant. g. Communicate with entrants. h. Monitor entry activities. i. Recording readings of the space as required. j. Summon rescue and emergency services. k. Prevent unauthorized entry. l. Perform non-entry rescue. m. Perform no additional duties. n. Remain alert and attentive to the space without distraction. 5.10.4 Duties of the Entry Supervisor: a. Know the potential hazards during entry and work. b. Determine if acceptable entry conditions are present at a permit space where entry is planned. c. Terminate entry as required by the standard. d. Verify that rescue services are readily available and the means for summoning them are operable. e. Remove unauthorized individuals who enter or try to enter the permit space during entry and work. f. Determine that entry and work operations remain consistent with entry permit terms and that acceptable entry conditions are maintained. g. The entry supervisor authorizing the entry may serve as an attendant for the entry. 5.11 Training 5.11.1 Procedures and duties will be reviewed with all affected employees prior to confined space entry. 5.11.2 Training will be conducted before the employee is first assigned duties. 5.11.3 Before a change in assigned duties. ∆ Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 18 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard 5.11.4 When a change in permit space operations. 5.11.5 Where there are deviations from the permit entry space procedures. 5.11.6 Where inadequacies in employee knowledge occur. 5.11.7 Training in job duties and the proper use of needed equipment, such as air monitoring equipment, will be given and proficiency established prior to entry and or on an annual basis. 5.11.8 Anyone who could serve as an attendant is also to be trained to use non-entry rescue equipment. 5.11.9 If rescue is to be done by a rescue team that will enter the space, they will receive: a. Training as required for equipment such as self-contained breathing apparatus and testing devices. b. Confined space hazard training the same as those entering the space. c. Annual rescue training includes both actual confined spaces and appropriate simulated representative confined spaces. d. Rescue team members will be trained in First aid and Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation (CPR). e. A copy of the training will include the materials and topics covered in the training. 5.11.10 Training must be documented as either initial or refresher and maintained for the period that all other training records are maintained (both immediately accessible or via archives) per each business unit’s practice. 5.12 Permission to Enter 5.12.1 No person is allowed to enter a confined space without permission. 5.12.2 No person is allowed to enter a confined space without a calibrated, functioning, personal four gas monitor that monitors O2, LEL, H2S, CO. 5.12.3 Entry means the point at which any part of a person's body breaks the plane into the opening of the space. 5.12.4 A copy of the complete Confined Space Permit, with attachments, must be posted or otherwise made highly visible at the entrance to a confined space before permission to enter is given. ∆ Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 19 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard 5.12.5 The Entry supervisor for a confined space must grant permission for entry by verifying required entry conditions and signing the permit. 5.12.6 Permission to enter a space can only be given after all aspects of the written entry permit, including attachments, have been met or completed and employees have completed training. 5.13 Canceling an Entry Permit 5.13.1 Permission to enter a confined space is canceled when: a. The task for which the permit was issued has been completed. b. A condition not allowed under the permit arises in or near the space. Any such problems or conditions and their resolution will be noted on the entry permit. c. The permit time limit has expired. 5.14 Monitoring 5.14.1 The level of air contaminants allowed will be evaluated by the Safety Department. This requirement is removed from the Standard. 5.14.2 Initial monitoring or atmospheric testing will be performed from outside the confined space prior to entry. 5.14.3 Initial Atmospheric Assessment will be completed prior to entry and completed from outside the space. 5.14.4 The air monitoring must be representative of the entire confined space including monitoring at the top, middle and bottom of the space to detect any stratification of the gases. 5.14.5 When a draw tube is used adequate time must be used at each level to allow the atmosphere to be drawn through the tube and into the monitor. 5.14.6 Oxygen (O2), Lower Explosive Limit (LEL), and Carbon Monoxide (CO) levels and H2S must be tested in ALL confined space entries at Cleveland-Cliffs. a. O2 (Oxygen) shall be between 19.5% and 23.5%. b. LEL (Lower Explosive Limit) will be less than 5%. c. CO (Carbon Monoxide) will be no greater than 35 PPM. d. H2S (Hydrogen Sulfide) will be no greater than 10 PPM. ∆ Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 20 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard 5.14.7 Monitoring must also be performed on any other hazard that may be present in the space. 5.14.8 Examples: HCN, Temperature, Ammonia, Radiation 5.14.9 Continuous Monitoring a. The attendant shall at intervals of no greater than 15 minutes monitor the space for changes in acceptable conditions. b. Communication will be established with each entrant based on the permissions to enter. c. Measure the atmosphere using a calibrated 4 gas monitor. d. Monitor for other hazards identified within the space. e. This will be recorded on the entry permit. 5.14.10 All entry operations shall be prohibited when any atmospheric level exceeds Cleveland-Cliffs requirements (<19.5% Oxygen, >23.5% Oxygen, 5% LEL, 35 PPM Carbon Monoxide, 10 PPM H2S). 5.14.11 All entry operations shall be ceased when any atmospheric level exceeds Cleveland-Cliffs requirements (<19.5% Oxygen, >23.5% Oxygen, 5% LEL, 35 PPM Carbon Monoxide, 10 PPM H2S). 5.14.12 Unless an alternative protective measure such as supplied air was determined to be required as a condition of entry. 5.14.13 Under special conditions for entry the conditions that prohibit the continued operation will be specified on the permit. 5.14.14 Results of the monitoring must be immediately provided to each entrant. 5.14.15 Each entrant must be provided with the opportunity to observe the pre- entry and any subsequent monitoring of the confined space. 5.15 Contractors 5.15.1 All contractors that take part in any aspect of confined space entry operations must, at a minimum, follow Cleveland-Cliffs Steel Confined Space Entry Procedures. Contractors must follow all Cleveland-Cliffs Steel documentation requirements involving Confined Space Entry Procedures. ∆ Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 21 of 22 Confined Space Safety Standard 5.15.2 The Cleveland-Cliffs Steel person responsible for bringing the contractor on site must notify the contractor about hazards of the confined space, Cleveland-Cliffs Steel monitoring requirements and Cleveland-Cliffs Steel Confined Space Entry Procedures. This notification must be documented. 5.15.3 The Cleveland-Cliffs Steel person responsible for bringing the contractor on site must ensure that the required debriefing is completed at the conclusion of the confined space entry. 5.15.4 Contractors shall provide their own monitors with proper calibration and a current bump test prior to the issue of a confined space permit. 6 Forms and Documented Information 6.1 Attachments / Forms 6.1.1 Confined Space Assessment Form Template (CCS-HSS-FRM-012.2) 6.1.2 Confined Space Permit Template (CCS-HSS-FRM-011.2) 7 Opportunities and Risks 7.1 This Standard is to ensure a consistent approach to confined space programs are used at all Cleveland-Cliffs Steelmaking facilities. 7.2 The Standard addresses: 7.2.1 Risk reduction. 7.2.2 Improved entry conditions. 7.2.3 Achieves desired conditions for entry. 7.2.4 Requires continued improvement. Energy Isolation Safety Standard outlines the responsibilities \nunder this standard, minimum verification procedure, training, and \nperiodic inspection requirements to protect people from potentially \nhazardous energy when servicing or performing maintenance on \nequipment, industrial vehicles, and machinery. \n1.2 When discussing hazardous energy, we mean any energy that can be \nreleased and might harm a person, such as: \n \nChemical \nTemperature \nGravity \nMotion \nMechanical \nElectrical \nPressure \nSound \nRadiation  \nBiological \n1.3 These are examples of hazardous energy being released and causing \nharm.  \n While workers are repairing a connection in piping, somewhere upstream of that \nrepair, another worker opens a valve sending fluids down the pipe that spills on and \nburns the maintenance workers. \n When a conveyor jams, a worker reaches in to try to clear the jam, when the jam is \nsuddenly and unexpectedly freed, the worker is crushed as a result. \n While a worker is servicing a machine, the internal wiring in the machine shorts, which \ncauses the maintenance worker to be shocked. \n1.4 Energy Isolation ensures these types of hazardous energy sources are \nisolated, controlled, or rendered in a zero-energy state to perform \nservicing or maintenance safely. \n1.5 This Energy Isolation Safety Standard applies to all Cleveland-Cliffs \nSteel employees, contractors, sub-contractors, service providers, \nvendors, and visitors when: \n Performing servicing and maintenance on equipment, industrial vehicles, or machines \nin which the energization or startup of the machines or equipment, or the release of \nstored energy, could harm employees regardless of the length of the exposure. \n \n\n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 3 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 3 of 52 \n Servicing and maintenance require the removal of machine guards or bypassing other \nsafety devices. \n Servicing and maintenance require personnel to place any part of their body into a \npoint of operation, an area on a machine, a piece of equipment where work is \nperformed, or into the danger zone associated with the machine's operation. \n1.6 This Energy Isolation Safety Standard does NOT apply to: \n Work on cord and plug connected electrical equipment if the equipment is unplugged \nfrom the energy source, and the authorized employee has exclusive control of the \nplug. \n Hot tapping operations. \n Energized electrical work for purposes of diagnostic work. \n Minor tool changes and adjustments and other minor servicing activities during \nnormal production operations. \n \n \n \n \n  \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 4 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 4 of 52 \n2 Responsibilities and Authorities \n2.1 This standard was jointly developed with participation from the \nrepresented workforce and management. \n2.2 Cleveland-Cliffs Steel is responsible for: \n Providing this This Energy Isolation Safety Standard. \n Complying with the various provisions of Federal, State, and Local rules and \nregulations. \n2.3 Plant General and Health and Safety Managers are responsible for: \n Establishing and maintaining guidance and implementation documents in compliance \nwith this Standard. \n Implementing the safety inspection process for new or modified equipment. \n Audit for compliance with this Standard. \n Ensuring lockout/tryout initial, annual and refresher training are available and \nconducted in compliance with this Standard. \n Investigating all lockout/tryout near misses, incidents, and injuries. \n2.4 Division Managers are responsible for: \n Ensuring that Section and Shift Managers are trained on all aspects of this Energy \nIsolation Standard. \n Ensuring employees attend energy Isolation training. \n Investigating all lockout/tryout near misses, incidents, and injuries. \n Ensuring that each energy isolation verification procedure receives a periodic \ninspection annually. \n2.5 Section Managers (Operations and Maintenance) are responsible for:  \n Ensuring correct lockout devices are available to control hazardous energy \nsuccessfully. \n Ensuring that the energy isolation verification procedures are developed and \nmaintained for jobs/tasks in their area of responsibility. \n Authorizing new or changed energy isolation verification procedures and compliance \nwith this Standard. \n Ensuring new or transferred employees receive hands-on training. \n Ensuring employees attend energy isolation training. \n Investigating all lockout/tryout near misses, incidents, and injuries. \n \n \n\n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 5 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 5 of 52 \n2.6 Shift Managers (Operations and Maintenance) are responsible for:  \n Ensuring employees are trained on applicable energy isolation and verification \nprocedures. \n Shift Managers authorize group lockout leaders utilizing the energy isolation \nAuthorized Employee Evaluation Form (CCS-HSS-FRM-0003.0). \n Ensuring group and individual lockouts are performed when required. \n Investigating all lockout/tryout near misses, incidents, and injuries. \n Ensuring that employees, contract personnel, and visitors comply with the \nrequirements detailed in this Standard. \n2.7 Employees are responsible for:  \n Reviewing and following energy isolation and verification procedures. \n Signing on to a group lockout box control list. \n Applying your personal safety lock to energy isolation devices, hasp, or a group \nlockout box. \n Personal safety lock removal when appropriate. \n Informing your manager if you have a concern with an energy isolation activity. \n Compliance with the use of three-part communications. \n Compliance with this Standard.  \n2.8 A Group Lockout Leader is: \n Experienced and knowledgeable of the machine/equipment operation. \n Capable of recognizing hazardous energy sources, the type and magnitude of the \nenergy available, and the methods and means necessary for energy isolation. \n An authorized employee selected by their Shift Manager who meets the Group \nLockout Leader evaluation criteria outlined in Form CCS-HSS-FRM-0003.0. \n The above evaluation is one and done unless a periodic inspection, observation, or \ninteraction reveals deviations or inadequacies in the employee's knowledge of Group \nLockout Leader duties. \n Trained to initiate energy isolation activities. \n Trained to apply locks to energy isolation devices, perform second-person verification \nand try out equipment, \n Trained to apply the control lock for the group lockout box and monitor completion of \nthe group lockout box control list, \n Not a permanent position unless the department makes it a permanent position. \n Responsible for assessing the equipment, facilitating the group energy isolation \nverification procedure, maintaining continuity throughout the energy isolation and \nactivity, and ensuring safe re-energization of the machine or equipment. \n2.8.1 It is essential to understand that the same person cannot apply the locks to energy \nisolation devices and verify the locks - Second Person Verification is required! \n2.8.2 Managers are to follow the training and retraining outlined in this document. \n \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 6 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 6 of 52 \n2.9 A Second Person Verifier is: \n Experienced and knowledgeable of the machine/equipment operation. \n Capable of recognizing hazardous energy sources, the type and magnitude of the \nenergy available, and the methods and means necessary for energy isolation. \n An employee selected and trained by their Shift Manager, to perform the duties \noutlined in this document. \n Managers are to follow the training and retraining outlined in this document. \n Responsible for walking down each assigned energy isolation device required to be \nlocked out according to the energy isolation verification procedure to assure \nequipment lock(s) were applied and secured to all identified energy isolation devices \n(Verify the Locks). \n Not a permanent position unless the department makes it a permanent position. \n Second Person Verification is required when multiple people, multiple energy \nsources, or group lockout boxes are used. \n Lockout involving just one authorized individual for a single energy source requires a \n“Tryout” of the equipment but does not require second-person verification. \n2.9.1 It is essential to understand that the same person cannot apply the locks and verify the \nlocks - Second Person Verification is required! \n2.10 A Tester/Tryout Person is: \n Experienced and knowledgeable of the machine/equipment operation. \n Capable of recognizing hazardous energy sources, the type and magnitude of the \nenergy available, and the methods and means necessary for energy isolation. \n An employee selected and trained by their Shift Manager to perform the duties \noutlined in this document.  \n Responsible for ensuring the area/equipment cleared of workers, the area cleared of \ntools/materials, machine guards reinstalled and secured, lockout device(s) removed, \ninforms affected employees, operates equipment (Tries Out), and returns controls to \nthe “off” or “neutral” position after the tryout. \n Not a permanent position unless the department makes it permanent.  \n2.11 Group Lockout Leaders, Second Person Verifiers, and Testers can \nbe cross-trained.  \n2.12 There shall be a master list maintained in each division of these \nauthorized employees trained for group lockout leaders, second-\nperson verifiers, and testers. Use form (CCS-HSS-FRM-0004.0, \nenergy isolation Training Log).  \n \n \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 7 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 7 of 52 \n2.12.1 Three-person example: Employee A (Group Lockout Leader) applies the locks, and \nEmployee B (Second Person Verifier) walks down the unit and verifies the locks. Then, \nEmployee C (Tester) tries out the equipment. \n2.12.2 Two-person example: Employee A (Group Lockout Leader) applies the locks, and \nEmployee B (Second Person Verifier) walks down the unit and verifies the locks. Then, \nEmployee A (Group Lockout Leader) or Employee B (Second Person Verifier) tries out \nthe equipment. \n2.13 Responsibility for Lock Placement and Lock Removal \n2.13.1 The employee placing a personal safety lock on the energy isolation device is \nresponsible for removing that lock. If the service being performed on the equipment \nhas not been completed at shift change, or if the employee is called off the job, that \nemployee must remove their personal safety lock(s). The incoming individual \ncontinuing the work must ensure that all energy sources are secured and properly \nlocked out. \n2.13.2 If it is necessary to cut off a personal safety lock that an employee or contract \nemployee failed or forgot to remove, approval must come from the Division Manager. \nA Safety Lock Removal Incident Report Form must be completed. \n2.13.3 A reasonable effort shall be made to contact the employee and ensure they are not at \nthe worksite. This effort shall consist of: \n Making phone or radio contact. \n Checking clock-out records and sign-in sheets. \n Thoroughly inspect the entire area to ensure the employee is not present. \n If necessary to cut the personal safety lock, the employee must be notified before \nreturning to work. \n2.13.4 Each employee is responsible for placing their personal safety lock on the correct \nenergy isolation device or group lockout box. \n2.13.5 The ultimate responsibility to recognize, lock, try and ensure all hazardous energy \nsources lie within each person who might be required to apply or abide by the \nprovisions of this Standard. \n \n  \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 8 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 8 of 52 \n3 References and Definitions \n3.1 References \n 29 CFR 1910.147 OSHA (Occupational Safety & Health Administration) Lockout \nStandard \n ANSI (American National Standards Institute) Z244.1-2016, the Control of Hazardous \nEnergy Lockout and Alternative Controls \n3.2 Definitions \n \nAlternative Controls (under the minor tool changes, adjustments, and minor servicing \nexemption): The means of controlling hazardous energy (other than energy isolation) to reduce \nrisk to an acceptable level. \n Alternative controls may include devices such as but are not limited to engineered \nsystems such as light curtains, pressure mats, area scanners, hold-to-run devices, \ninterlock switches, stop devices, trapped key devices, specially designed tools, \nremote devices, interlocked barrier guards, local disconnects or keyed control \nswitches, restraint devices that prevent the employee from entering the zone of \ndanger, and Plexiglas or other physical and substantial protective barriers in a fixed \nposition preventing the employee's body from entering the machine hazard area and \nadjacent equipment that could cause harm. \n Before energy isolation is implemented on a unit, all alternative control devices shall \nbe checked and verified that the control keys, switches, or interlocks are in their \nproper position and the unit is in normal operations mode. This ensures that no \nalternative controls interrupt normal operation and do not interfere with the energy \nisolation and verification. \nAuthorized Employee: An employee who locks out machines or equipment to perform \nservicing or maintenance. \nAffected Employee: An employee who is required to use machines or equipment on which \nservicing or maintenance is being performed under the energy isolation standard or who \nperforms other job responsibilities in an area where such servicing or maintenance is \nperformed. An affected employee becomes an authorized employee when that employee's \nduties include servicing or maintenance. \nBlind: Is the absolute closure of a pipe, line, or duct achieved by fastening a solid plate, \nthreaded plug, or cap across its bore to cover it completely. The cover must:  \nA.  At least cover the outer edge of a flange’s mating surface. \nB.  Be capable of withstanding the maximum upstream system pressure during normal \noperations.  \nC.  Blinds include blanks, slip plates, blind flanges, threaded caps, and physical \ndisconnects. \n The three types of blinds utilized include: \n \nA.  Isolation \nB.  Hydrotest \nC.  Permanent Blinds must be capable of holding the maximum pressure and \ntemperature to which the blind will be exposed. \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 9 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 9 of 52 \n Examples of acceptable handles are the “Spectacle,” \"T handle,” or \"J handle\" design. \n To eliminate the possibility of confusing restriction orifices with blinds, blinds with straight \ntabs must not be used. \n \nFigure 1 (above) - Blind-Flange Lockout Device \n \n The crew securing the pipeline locks out upstream valves and then places a blank or \nblind in the system to positively block any potential downstream flow. \n The flange bolts are then torqued to proper specification. At least one torqued bolt is \ncovered with the blind-flange lockout device (see Figure 1 above). \n The device is secured with one or more personal safety locks, or a group lockbox could \nbe used. Multiple bolts could be covered using multiple devices if desired. \nCapable of Being Locked Out: An energy-isolating device is considered capable of being \nlocked out if: \n It is designed with a hasp or other means of attachment to which a lock can be affixed. \n It has a locking mechanism built into it. \n It can be locked without dismantling, rebuilding, replacing the energy-isolating device, \nor permanently altering its energy control capability. \nControl Lock (Group Lockout Box): A yellow, keyed lock having only one key is used to \nsecure the group lockout box to provide continuity of a lockout for multiple energy sources, \nmultiple crews, or across shifts. \n Control locks shall be engraved with the division’s name and \"Control Lock.\" \nDanger Zone: The danger zone is the machine’s point of operation area from which the \noperator must stay clear during the machine’s operation cycle to prevent injury (caught in, burn, \nshock, etc.). \nEnergized: Machines and equipment are energized when connected to an energy source or \nwhen they may contain residual or stored energy. \n\n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 10 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 10 of 52 \nDe-Energized: Disconnected from all energy sources and not containing residual or stored \nenergy. \nDouble Block and Bleed: Closing a line, duct, or pipe by closing and locking two inline valves \nand opening and locking a drain or vent valve in the line between the two closed valves. \nEnergy-Isolating Device: A mechanical device that physically prevents the transmission or \nrelease of energy, including but not limited to the following:  \n A manually operated electrical circuit breaker. \n A disconnect switch. \n A manually operated switch by which the conductors of a circuit can be disconnected \nfrom all ungrounded supply conductors, and in addition, no pole can be operated \nindependently. \n A valve. \n A block and any similar restraint device are used to block or secure stored energy \nsources or prevent equipment and machinery from accidental movement. \n NOTE: Push buttons, selector switches, wall switches, and other control circuit type \ndevices are not energy isolating devices. \nA. All energy isolation devices shall be labeled or marked to indicate their \nfunction. The identification shall include the following: \n1. Machine, equipment, or process supplied; and \n2. Energy type and magnitude. \nB. The marking shall be of sufficient durability to withstand the anticipated \nenvironment. \nC. The potential for injury and incidents may be reduced if employees are not \nexpected to rely on memory as to which isolating devices apply, particularly in \ncomplex equipment. There is a greater potential for error when unlabeled or \ninadequately identified energy-isolating devices exist. \nD. Examples of marking and labeling include attached or embossed markings \nand signs such as: \n “Main Breaker (480 V) Press 3” \n “Natural Gas Shutoff (20 psi) Process Line 2” \n “Drive Power Isolation (120V), Axis X, Only” \n “High Pressure (600 psi) Return Line\" \nE. Electrical boxes must be labeled directly on the box. Valves may be labeled on \nthe valve body or a suspended durable sign or tag. Stencil, color, tags, or \nother methods may be labeled by constraints, blocks, chains, or pins. \nEnergy Source: Any chemical, temperature, gravity, motion, mechanical, electrical, pressure, \nsound, radiation, biological, or other energy sources. \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 11 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 11 of 52 \nEquipment Lock: A blue, keyed lock having only one key is used to secure an energy \nisolation device on machines or equipment. \nEquipment locks shall be marked with terminology matching the energy isolation device \n(e.g., #5 Scrap Baler Hydraulic Supply Valve).  \n Because energy isolation device terminology can be lengthy, it is permitted to number \nequipment locks sequentially if a legend exists describing each equipment lock, and a \nlegend is provided with each energy isolation. \n The energy isolation verification procedure can be the legend. \n In place of marking equipment locks, a durable tag with a grommet can be attached to the \nshank of an equipment lock to identify the equipment lock. \nGrounding: Conducting connection between an electrical circuit or equipment and the earth. \nGroup Lockout: A lockout that occurs when one or more employees perform servicing or \nmaintenance on equipment with multiple energy sources simultaneously.  \nGroup Lockout Box: A storage device with a see-through front used in group lockout to secure \nall keys to the equipment locks secured on an energy isolating device(s) and permits all \nexposed persons to apply their personal safety lock. \nHazardous Energy: Any chemical, temperature, gravity, motion, mechanical, electrical, \npressure, sound, radiation, and biological or other energy can harm personnel. Some energy \nsources are apparent such as electricity, heat in a furnace, or something that might fall. Others \nmay be hidden hazards such as air pressure in a system or a tightly wound spring. \nHot tapping or pressure tapping connects existing piping or pressure vessels without \ninterrupting or emptying that section of pipe or vessel. As a result, the pipe or tank can continue \noperating while maintenance or modifications are being done. The process is also used to drain \noff pressurized casing fluids and add test points or sensors such as temperature and pressure. \n Hot taps can range from a ½-inch hole designed for something as simple as quality \ncontrol testing to a 48-inch tap to install a variety of ports, valves, t-sections, or other \npipes. \n Hot tap operations that involve transmission and distribution systems for gas, steam, \nwater, or petroleum products on pressurized pipelines, if continuity of service is essential, \nthe shutdown of the system is impractical, documented procedures are followed, and \nemployees are effectively protected by special equipment. \nIdled Equipment: Equipment no longer in use or will be out of service beyond a month. This \nequipment requires that a green lock be applied. \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 12 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 12 of 52 \n Do Not Operate (DNO) tags can no longer be used as the sole means for designating \nout-of-service equipment.  \nIndustrial Vehicles: includes all powered industrial trucks (PIT), fork trucks, tractors, platform \nlift trucks, trucks, cars, motorized personnel carts, material handling wheeled or tracked \nindustrial vehicles, and other specialized industrial trucks powered by electric motors or internal \ncombustion engines used exclusively in the industrial setting. The term \"Industrial Vehicle\" will \nmean all the above. \nLockout: The placement of a lockout device on an energy-isolating device so that the \ndevice and the equipment being controlled cannot be operated until the lockout device is \nremoved.  Lockout is not completed and ready for individuals to add their safety lock to the lock \nbox until verification and try-out are completed and recorded on the energy isolation and \nverification procedure. \nLockout Device: Any device that uses a positive means such as blank flanges, bolted slip \nblinds, and other devices to hold an energy-isolating device safely before a standard lock is \napplied, thereby preventing the energizing of machinery or equipment. \n \n See Figures 2, 3, and 4 below for other lockout devices (e.g., electrical, motor vehicle, \nvalve, cable): \n \nFigure 2 (above) - Example of Electrical and Industrial Vehicle Lockout Devices \n \nFigure 3 (above) - Example of Valve Lockout Devices \n\n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 13 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 13 of 52 \n \nFigure 4 (above) - Example of Cable Lockout Devices \nLockout Hasp: A device that provides effective multi-person lockout on an energy isolation \ndevice or group lockout box (see Figure 5 below). Multiple personal safety locks can be \napplied to a lockout hasp, allowing a single energy isolation device to be isolated by more \nthan one worker or multiple personal safety locks to a group lockout box. \n \nFigure 5 – Example of Lockout Hasp \nEnergy Isolation) Verification Procedure: A written, standardized and controlled procedure \nprepared for a piece of equipment or process system detailing the correct lockout/tryout \nverification process for hazardous energy, including residual and stored energy (see form \nCCS-HSS-FRM-0001.0). \nNormal Production Operations: Utilize a machine or equipment to perform its intended \nproduction function. \nOther Employees: All employees who may be in an area where energy isolation and \nverification procedures may be utilized. \nPersonal Safety Lock: A red, keyed lock having only one key and used only by the trained and \nauthorized employee; the lock was assigned to ensure personal protection during lockout/tryout \nenergy isolation activities. \n Personal safety locks must be marked with the employee's name, payroll, clock, or badge \nnumber. \n \n\n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 14 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 14 of 52 \nPlug-Controlled Equipment: Cord and plug connected electric equipment for which exposure \nto the hazards of energization or startup of the equipment is controlled by unplugging the \nequipment from the energy source. \nPoint-of-Operation: The area on a machine where work is performed upon the material being \nprocessed. The point of operation of machines whose operation exposes an employee to injury \nshall be guarded. \nSecond-Person Verification: A second authorized individual (the Verifier) shall verify that each \nequipment lock is placed and secured correctly on each energy isolation device. \n Second Person Verification is required when multiple people, multiple energy sources, or \ngroup lockout boxes are used. \nA. “Lockout-Verify the Locks-Tryout.” \n Lockout involving just one authorized individual for a single energy source requires a \n“Tryout” of the equipment but does not require second person verification or an energy \nisolation and verification procedure. \nServicing and Maintenance: Workplace activities such as constructing, installing, setting up, \nadjusting, inspecting, modifying, maintaining, and servicing machines or equipment, including \nlubrication, cleaning or unjamming of machines or equipment, and adjusting or tool changes \nwhere employees could be exposed to the energization or startup of the equipment or release of \nhazardous energy. \nSetting Up: Any work performed to prepare a machine or equipment to perform its normal \nproduction operation. \nUnder Exclusive Control: The authorized employee would be able to prevent the equipment \nfrom becoming reenergized during their servicing or maintenance of the equipment. Plug-\ncontrolled equipment is under the exclusive control of the employee if the plug is physically \nin possession of the employee, within arm’s reach and in line of sight of the employee, or if the \nemployee has affixed a personal safety lock on the plug. \nTags for Tagout: The placement of a tag only on an energy-isolating device is prohibited for \nenergy isolation unless the energy isolation device does not accommodate a lock (see Section \n21). \nTags for Lock Identification: \n Substantial tags with grommets can be used on contractor personal safety locks for \noutages and travelers. \n Tags can be attached to the shank of equipment locks if you choose not to engrave the \nequipment locks. The markings on the tag or equipment lock must be legible and match \nthe identification of the energy isolation device. \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 15 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 15 of 52 \nThree-Part Communication: Anytime more than one person or crew is involved in a task that \nrequires understanding and coordination between the two, clear and concise Initiate-\nAcknowledge-Confirm (IAC) communication shall be utilized. \n A key to excellent communication is establishing procedures and a culture that utilizes \nInitiate-Acknowledge-Confirm 100% of the time. \n With three-part communication, a mishap can be avoided: \nEmployee A – entry end operator: \n“Are your hands out of the way?” \nEmployee B – removing equipment locks after a maintenance turn: \n“My hands are clear; go ahead and bump the mandrel rewind motor.” \nEmployee A – entry end operator: \n“Your hands are clear; now bumping the mandrel rewind motor.” \nTryout: Tryout requires that you physically attempt to turn on all power switches and devices \nonce the equipment has been locked out. The tryout is your final check and assurance that the \nequipment has been isolated from all power sources. Tryout can be accomplished in the \nfollowing ways: \n Attempting to operate the equipment (manually shifting operating valves, turning on \nswitches, etc.). \n Visually verifying that needed safeguards are in place or that blades in the corresponding \ndisconnect box are disengaged. \n Using a voltmeter or other test device. \n Some lockout/tryout activities will require all three tryout methods to ensure that energy \nsources are properly locked out. \nZero Energy State: means a condition reached when all energy sources to or within equipment \nare isolated, blocked, or otherwise relieved with no possibility of re-accumulation. \n \n  \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 16 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 16 of 52 \n4 Resources \n4.1  \n \n \n \n \n \n  \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 17 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 17 of 52 \n5 Requirements \n5.1 Cleveland-Cliffs shall provide training to ensure that employees \nunderstand the purpose and function of the lockout/tryout energy \nisolation standard and that the knowledge and skill required for the \nsafe application, usage, and removal of the energy isolations are \nacquired by employees. \n5.2 The Health, Safety, and Security Manager or designee will conduct \ninitial training during new hire orientation.  \n5.3 Next, hands-on training will be conducted by the employee’s Shift \nManager and trained co-workers.  \n5.3.1 This Energy Isolation Safety Standard will be presented at least annually during a \nsafety meeting by an authorized manager.  \n Refresher training shall also occur when a periodic inspection reveals deviations or \ninadequacies in the employee's knowledge or use of the energy isolation verification \nprocedure(s). \n5.4 Training shall include the following: \n5.4.1 Authorized Employees – Training in recognizing applicable hazardous energy sources, \nthe type and magnitude of the energy available in their workplace, and the methods \nand means necessary for energy isolation. \n5.4.2 Affected Employees – Training will include the purpose and use of the energy isolation \nverification procedures. \n5.4.3 Other Employees – Training will include instruction on the energy isolation verification \nprocedures and the prohibition relating to restarting or reenergizing machines or \nequipment which are locked out. \n5.5 Employee retraining is required: \n5.5.1 Whenever an employee is transferred, or there is a change in their job assignments (a \nposition change that requires a new job orientation). \n5.5.2 Whenever a new hazard is introduced due to a change in machines, equipment, or \nprocess. \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 18 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 18 of 52 \n5.5.3 Whenever there is a change in energy isolation verification procedures. \n5.5.4 Whenever a periodic inspection reveals inadequacies in the company's energy \nisolation verification procedures or the employee's knowledge of the energy isolation \nverification procedures. \n5.6 This Energy Isolation Safety Standard shall be covered with \nemployees yearly in a safety meeting(s). \n5.6.1 Training must be documented with the employee's printed name, signature, employee \nID number, date of training, and the printed name and signature of the person \nconducting the training. Use Training Log (CCS-HSS-FRM-0004.0). \n5.7 Seven Steps to Safe Equipment Shutdown \n The following procedure shall be utilized to lockout and try out hazardous energy sources \nto achieve a zero-energy state when performing servicing and maintenance so that \nmaintenance and servicing can be performed safely. \n5.7.1 Hazardous Energy Assessment – Before beginning work on equipment, perform a \nTAKE 5 to assess the equipment to determine the energy sources and the appropriate \nenergy isolating devices. \n Assess potentially hazardous stored or residual energy and initiate measures to relieve, \ndisconnect, restrain or otherwise render “safe” any stored or residual energy. Ensure re-\naccumulation of stored energy is not possible. \n Consult the applicable energy isolation verification procedure. \n5.7.2 Notification – Notify all affected employees that an energy isolation procedure will be \nutilized. Do not proceed with the lockout until authorized by the equipment operator(s). \n5.7.3 Shutdown – Shut down or turn off the machine or equipment using normal or \nemergency shutdown procedures established for the machine or equipment. \n5.7.4 Isolate – Physically locate and isolate all energy isolating devices needed to control \nthe energy to the machine or the equipment. \n5.7.5 Lockout – Authorized employees shall place and secure applicable lockout devices to \neach energy-isolating device identified in the energy isolation and verification \nprocedure. Place your personal safety lock on the lockout device(s), locking them in a \n\"safe\" or zero energy position. In the case of a group lockout, place your personal \nsafety lock on the group lockout box. \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 19 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 19 of 52 \n5.7.6 Second Person Verification – Physically walk down each energy isolation device to \nverify that an equipment lock(s) is applied and secured correctly to each energy \nisolation device. \n Second Person Verification is required when multiple people, multiple energy sources, or \ngroup lockout boxes are used. \n Lockout involving just one authorized individual for a single energy source requires a \n“Tryout” of the equipment but does not require second-person verification. \n Second Person Verification can be accomplished simultaneously after the equipment \nlocks are placed and secured. \n5.7.7 Tryout – Before starting servicing or maintenance, ensure all employees are in a safe \nlocation. Tryout can be accomplished through the following acts: \n Attempting to operate the equipment. \n Visually verifying that needed safeguards are in place or that blades in the corresponding \ndisconnect box are disengaged.  \n Using a voltmeter or other test device. \n Some lockout/tryout activities will require all three tryout methods to ensure that energy \nsources are properly locked out. \nNOTE: If the machine/equipment operates or is not in a zero-energy state, notify your \nShift Manager immediately and do not service or perform any work tasks on the \nequipment until isolation can be verified. \n5.8 Seven Steps to Safe Equipment Start-Up \n The procedure listed below shall be utilized to return lockout equipment to normal \noperation safely. \n5.8.1 Inspect – Inspect the work area for personnel, ensure all guards have been reinstalled, \nall tools and materials have been removed, and the equipment is ready for normal \noperation. \n5.8.2 Notify – Notify all affected employees and operator(s) in the work area that the \nequipment will be reenergized. \n5.8.3 Complete – Complete the Post energy isolation activity checklist. \n5.8.4 Confirm – Confirm that all employees are in a safe location and clear from the \nequipment. \n5.8.5 Remove – Remove your lock and lockout hasp from the energy isolation device(s) or \ngroup lockout box or any blanks or blinds on piping. \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 20 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 20 of 52 \n5.8.6 Restore – Restore energy to the machine/equipment. Visually verify that the area is \nclear of personnel and tools and that guards are in place and secure before restoring \nenergy. \n5.8.7 Inform – Inform all employees and operators that the equipment is ready for normal \noperation. \n5.9 Standardized Lock, Lock Purpose, and Lock Identification \n RED = Personal safety locks (Cliffs and Contractor) \n BLUE = Equipment locks \n YELLOW = Control and cross locks for group lockout boxes  \n GREEN = Idled equipment locks (green locks are not to be used for protecting \npersonnel performing servicing or maintenance) \n5.9.1 The standard lock for all Cleveland-Cliffs Steel: \n An American Standard keyed lock. \n Locks can have either 1-1/2 inch or 3-inch shanks.  \n Only used to secure hazardous energy sources (except green), and those locks shall not \nbe used for any other purpose. \n5.9.2 There shall be no duplicate or additional keys for personal safety, equipment, or \ncontrol locks. \n5.9.3 Any secondary keys must be destroyed. \n5.9.4 All attempts will be made to eliminate the ability to order locks with more than one key \nthrough procurement practices. \n5.9.5 The only exceptions to the standard lock and lock identification rule are designed \nenergy control devices like the Kirk Key or Castell Locking Systems. \n5.9.6 Written permission from the Health, Safety and Security Department must be obtained \nbefore using such an energy control device. \n5.9.7 Personal Safety Lock: A red, keyed lock having only one key, used only by the trained \nand authorized employee the lock was assigned to ensure personal protection during \nlockout/tryout energy isolation activities. \n Employees shall carry their personal safety lock while at work as part of their standard \nPPE (Personal Protective Equipment). \n Each employee is responsible for keeping the key to their assigned personal safety lock \nin their possession.  \n5.9.8 A personal safety lock shall not be applied or removed by anyone else.  \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 21 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 21 of 52 \n5.9.9 Personal safety locks must be marked with at least the employee's name and one \nother form of identification such as department name, department identification \nnumber, clock/badge number, or specific crew. \n5.9.10 Employees shall have possession of their personal safety lock key during the entire \ntime they are locked out on a piece of equipment. \n5.9.11 At no time shall any employee enter a protected area without possession of a key that \ncontrols the energy sources. \n5.9.12 There is no limit to the number of personal safety locks an employee can be issued. \n5.9.13 Equipment Lock: A blue, keyed lock having only one key, used to secure energy \nisolation devices on machines or equipment to isolate energy sources following \nestablished, written energy isolation verification procedures.  \n5.9.14 Equipment locks shall be engraved or have a durable tag with a grommet attached to \nthe equipment lock shank with terminology matching the energy isolation device \n(example #5 Scrap Baler Hydraulic Supply Valve). \n5.9.15 Because energy isolation device terminology can be lengthy, it is permitted to number \nequipment locks sequentially if a legend exists describing each equipment lock. A \nlegend shall be provided with each energy isolation. The energy isolation and \nverification procedure should be the legend. \n5.9.16 The corresponding equipment lock key number must match the equipment lock \nnumber. This is important so all elements of the energy isolation can be verified. \n5.9.17 The equipment lock key number must be visible from the outside of the group lockout \nbox. \n5.9.18 It is permissible to attach a durable identification tag to the key to be visible outside the \ngroup lockout box. \n5.9.19 An equipment lock must be attached to each personal protective ground wire installed. \nThe lock reminds you that ground wires are applied to crane hot rails or other devices \nto reduce the likelihood of powering on with the grounds still applied. \n5.9.20 Control or Cross Lock: A yellow, keyed lock having only one key used to secure the \ngroup lockout box to provide continuity of a lockout for multiple energy sources, \nmultiple crews, or across shifts. \n \n \n \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 22 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 22 of 52 \n The control lock is applied to the group lockout box after all equipment lock keys have \nbeen placed in the group lockout box. Before applying personal safety locks, the control \nlock is secured on the group lockout box. The control lock shall not be removed until all \npersonal safety locks have been removed from the box; therefore, the control lock is the \nfirst lock on the group lockout box and the last lock to be removed. \n5.9.21 All group lockout boxes at Cleveland-Cliffs Steel shall have a control lock applied.  \n5.9.22 Control locks shall be engraved with the division name and \"Control Lock.\" \n5.9.23 The control lock shall be identified on the Locked Key Box Control List by writing \n\"Control Lock\" followed by the authorized employee's name who placed the lock.  \n5.9.24 When a shift change occurs, and the authorized person holding the control lock key \nneeds to leave the facility, that control lock key shall be passed to the oncoming \nauthorized person. In addition, the control lock shall be identified on the Group Lockout \nBox Control List by writing \"Control Lock,\" followed by the new authorized individual's \nname who now controls the control lock key. The previous control lock entry on the \nLocked Key Box Control List shall be dated, time-stamped, and lined out by the \noutgoing authorized person (see Figure 6 below). \n \n \nFigure 6 (above) - Group Lockout Box Control List \n\n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 23 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 23 of 52 \n5.9.25 The passing of the control lock key and documenting the change of control for the \ncontrol lock key continues as necessary (multiple shift changes) until the lockout/tryout \nactivity is complete.  \n5.9.26 The control lock key is the only key that may be passed from one authorized person to \nthe following authorized person. A control lock shall be used on every lockout activity \nwhere a lockbox is utilized. \n5.9.27 Idled Equipment or Do Not Operate Locks (Green) locks shall be used only to control \nidled equipment and equipment you do not want someone to operate. \n5.9.28 Green locks are not to be used to protect personnel for servicing or maintenance. \n5.9.29 If the machine or equipment is going to be idled beyond a month, apply a green lock to \nthe applicable energy isolation devices. \n If you do not want personnel to use the machine or equipment, apply a green lock and \n“Do Not Operate” tag. \n5.9.30 All locks shall be purchased only through the approved safety vendor. \n5.10 Lockout Hasp and Group Lockout Box \n5.10.1 Lockout Hasp: A lockout hasp is a multiple lock device. \n A lockout hasp is ideal when more than one person works on machinery or equipment to \nisolate a single energy isolation device. \n The lockout hasp is attached to the equipment's energy isolation device, and each person \nlocking out applies their personal safety lock to the lockout hasp. \n A control lock is not required for a hasp unless a hasp is used on a group lockout box. \n If you approach a lockout hasp and only one hole is remaining to place your personal \nsafety lock, avoid applying your personal safety lock; instead, add another lockout hasp \nto that hole, and then apply your personal safety lock to the newly attached lockout hasp \nso additional personnel can apply their personal safety lock. \n5.10.2 There are situations where using a group lockout box is more practical than a lockout \nhasp, such as when multiple energy sources need to be isolated. \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 24 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 24 of 52 \n5.10.3 Group Lockout Box (Figure 7, right): A container with a see-\nthrough front used in group lockout to secure all keys to the \nequipment locks secured on an energy isolating device(s) \nand permits all exposed persons to apply their personal \nsafety lock. \n5.10.4 Group lockout boxes are used when more than one \nemployee and more than one energy source are isolated.  \n5.10.5 Lockboxes shall be arranged, organized, and labeled regardless of whether they are \nmounted on the wall or placed near the locked and tried-out machine or equipment to \ncreate one convenient location for all involved to identify the lockboxes readily. This \narrangement (see Figures 7 above and 8 below) is called a lockout station. \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \nFigure 8 (above) – This is the lockout box you see on the far left in Figure 7. This \nlockout box has hooks numbered to match each energy-isolation device to lockout \nequipment MD1. The hooks house an equipment lock (if not locked out) or the key to \nthat equipment lock (if locked out). \n5.10.6 A Group lockout box's purpose is to ensure that the equipment lock keys cannot be \naccessed until the control lock has been removed. The control lock's purpose is to \nmaintain the established lockout/tryout integrity during multiple shifts, shift changes, \nand throughout the lockout activity. \n IMPORTANT: To maintain the integrity of an established lockout/tryout activity, a control \nlock shall be the first lock placed on a group lockout box after equipment lock keys are \nplaced in the group lockout box and the last lock removed from the group lockout box. \n \n \n \n \n \n\n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 25 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 25 of 52 \n5.10.7 Group lockout boxes must have a see-through face so that the number of keys and the \nidentity of the keys is visible from outside the box. The see-through group lockout box \nrequirement applies to all group lockout boxes (fixed location and portable) used at \nCleveland-Cliffs Steel. \n5.10.8 Group lockout boxes must have the Group Lockout Box Control List attached.
